Yeah… I mean, it feels like it’s most days now. Ever since those foreclosure notices started coming, I feel like I’m just coming apart at the seams. Even if nothing is happening right that second, I’m still sitting there waiting for the next bad thing. And I’m making stupid mistakes in the repair bay—things I never would’ve messed up before all this. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yeah, I have. Those exact things, actually. I used to get something out of them, not just because they were “activities,” but because they made me feel like myself. Church volunteering gave me a sense that I was useful, like I was showing up for people. Fitness classes made me feel strong and steady. And the truck-club outings were just… fun, you know? Being around people who understood that side of me, talking engines and builds and just having something to look forward to.

Now I mostly don’t go. I keep meaning to. I’ll think, “Okay, this week I’m going to get back to class,” or “I should show up at church, people are probably wondering where I am,” but then when it’s time, it just feels empty or like too much effort for something I’m not even sure I’ll enjoy. Same with the truck-club stuff — I used to be excited about it, and now I’ll see messages about an outing and just let it pass.

And it’s not only outside work. At work they’ve taken me out of the high-risk repair bay, and that’s been really hard because that was work I was trusted with. I took pride in that. So losing that, and then not showing up for the things I used to care about after work… it makes me feel like I’ve kind of dropped out of my own life.

Ever since the foreclosure notices started showing up, I just… I haven’t been myself at all. I’ll be sitting in my truck before work crying, trying to pull it together before I go into the shop, and then the phone rings and I jump like something terrible is about to happen.\n\nAt work, I’m snapping at the apprentices over little things, and that’s not how I usually am. I work on heavy equipment, so you can’t be distracted or careless, and I’ve been making dumb mistakes in the repair bay—mistakes I never would’ve made before all this started. It’s like my nerves are shot and I can’t get my footing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried most days, even when there isn’t a specific problem happening at that moment?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it feels like it’s most days now. Ever since those foreclosure notices started coming, I feel like I’m just coming apart at the seams. Even if nothing is happening right that second, I’m still sitting there waiting for the next bad thing. Like I’ll be in my truck before work and I just start crying, trying to get myself together before I walk into the shop.\n\nAnd then the phone rings and I jump like it’s going to be more terrible news. I know that probably sounds ridiculous, but my whole body reacts before I can even think. At work I’m on edge, too. I’ve been snapping at the apprentices over little stuff, and I hate that because that’s not really me. And I’m making stupid mistakes in the repair bay—things I never would’ve messed up before all this. It’s like I can’t shut the worry off, even when there isn’t an actual problem happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or hopeless most days, or have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not just that I feel sad, it’s more like everything that used to keep me steady has just kind of fallen away. I used to be really involved outside of work — church volunteering, my fitness classes, even the truck-club outings. Those were things I showed up for without even thinking about it, like they were part of who I was. Lately I just… don’t go. I’ll tell myself I’m going to, and then I don’t get out the door.\n\nAnd at work it’s gotten bad enough that they’ve taken me out of the high-risk repair bay, which was embarrassing. I’ve definitely had stretches where I felt really low and just… heavy, like I couldn’t see how things were going to get better. But “nearly every day for most of the day” and “two weeks straight” — I don’t know if I can say that exactly.\n\nSome days it’s there from the minute I wake up, and it follows me around all day. Other days I’ll get distracted for a little while or I’ll be functional enough to get through what I have to do, and then it hits me again later. So I don’t want to overstate it and say yes if you mean literally most of the day every day for two full weeks. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. It’s been bad, but I’m not sure it fits that exact pattern.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, was there ever a time when you felt down or hopeless almost every day, all day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know if I can say that, not the way you’re wording it. I mean, I can look back and say there were times I felt really down, like it was hanging over me most of the time, and I’d wake up already dreading the day. But “almost every day, all day, for two weeks in a row” feels hard for me to pin down.\n\nI wasn’t exactly keeping track, you know? Some days felt completely awful, and then other days maybe I’d have a few hours where I was distracted or just pushing through, and I don’t know whether that counts or not. So I don’t want to say yes and make it sound more exact than I really remember. It’s been bad, but I’m not sure I can honestly say it was all day, nearly every day, for a full two weeks straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer get pleasure or satisfaction out of things you used to enjoy, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings?\n\nPatient: Yes… yeah, I have. Those exact things, actually. I used to get something out of them, not just because they were “activities,” but because they made me feel like myself. Being around people who understood that side of me, talking engines and builds and just having something to look forward to.\n\nNow I mostly don’t go. I keep meaning to. I’ll think, “Okay, this week I’m going to get back to class,” or “I should show up at church, people are probably wondering where I am,” but then when it’s time, it just feels empty or like too much effort for something I’m not even sure I’ll enjoy. Same with the truck-club stuff — I used to be excited about it, and now I’ll see messages about an outing and just let it pass.\n\nAnd it’s not only outside work. At work they’ve taken me out of the high-risk repair bay, and that’s been really hard because that was work I was trusted with. I took pride in that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I still don’t know that I can answer that with a clean yes. I understand you’re giving me a little more room there, like not absolutely every single day, but even then… I’m hesitant.

There have been periods where it felt like I was down most of the time, where the day had this gray cast over it and I was kind of dragging myself through. And if you asked me in the middle of one of those weeks, I probably would’ve said, “Yeah, I’m pretty much miserable.” But looking back and trying to say it was most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks in a row — I’m just not sure.

I can remember bad days grouped together. I can remember waking up already feeling defeated. But I can also remember times where I’d get pulled into something or have a stretch where I was functioning, or not thinking about it as much, and then later I’d crash back into feeling awful. So I don’t know how to count that.

I don’t want to dodge the question. It’s been really low at times. But I don’t feel confident saying, “Yes, definitely, that happened for at least two weeks straight.” I just don’t have that level of certainty about it.

Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. Maybe “completely” is a strong word, because there might be little moments where I’m distracted or I can appreciate something in theory, but for a week or more at a time, yes — the things that used to actually give me satisfaction just haven’t felt worth showing up for.

I’ve stopped going to church volunteering, and that bothers me because I used to be pretty consistent with it. It wasn’t just something on the calendar — it made me feel useful, like I was part of something. Same with fitness classes. I used to keep up with them, and now I just don’t go. And the truck-club outings… I used to really look forward to those, and now I’ll see the messages or know there’s something happening, and I just let it pass.

And work is part of it too. I’ve been taken out of the high-risk repair bay, and that has hit me hard. That was the kind of work I took pride in, where I felt trusted and capable. So between that and not showing up for the things outside of work that used to matter to me, it really does feel like I’ve lost a lot of what made me feel like myself.

I understand you’re giving me a little more room there, like not absolutely every single day, but even then… I’m hesitant.\n\nThere have been periods where it felt like I was down most of the time, where the day had this gray cast over it and I was kind of dragging myself through. And if you asked me in the middle of one of those weeks, I probably would’ve said, “Yeah, I’m pretty much miserable.” But looking back and trying to say it was most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks in a row — I’m just not sure.\n\nI can remember bad days grouped together. I can remember waking up already feeling defeated. But I can also remember times where I’d get pulled into something or have a stretch where I was functioning, or not thinking about it as much, and then later I’d crash back into feeling awful. So I don’t know how to count that.\n\nI don’t want to dodge the question. It’s been really low at times. But I don’t feel confident saying, “Yes, definitely, that happened for at least two weeks straight.” I just don’t have that level of certainty about it.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you completely stopped getting any enjoyment or satisfaction from things you used to really like, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings — even for a week or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. Maybe “completely” is a strong word, because there might be little moments where I’m distracted or I can appreciate something in theory, but for a week or more at a time, yes — the things that used to actually give me satisfaction just haven’t felt worth showing up for.\n\nI’ve stopped going to church volunteering, and that bothers me because I used to be pretty consistent with it. It wasn’t just something on the calendar — it made me feel useful, like I was part of something. Same with fitness classes. I used to keep up with them, and now I just don’t go. And the truck-club outings… I used to really look forward to those, and now I’ll see the messages or know there’s something happening, and I just let it pass.\n\nAnd work is part of it too. I’ve been taken out of the high-risk repair bay, and that has hit me hard. That was the kind of work I took pride in, where I felt trusted and capable. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I can say “absolutely no” for two weeks or more. That wording is hard for me, because I don’t know that I’ve been completely flat, like there was zero interest or zero pleasure every single day for that long.

What I can say is I’ve had stretches where those things felt so distant from me that I just didn’t go, or I didn’t want to go enough to make myself do it. Like I’d see something about the truck-club outing and instead of feeling that pull — the “oh, that’ll be fun” feeling — it was more like, “I can’t deal with that.” Or with fitness classes, I’d think about going and then it would feel pointless before I even got my shoes on. Same with volunteering — I’d know it mattered, but I didn’t feel connected to it the way I used to.

But if you’re asking specifically, “Was there a solid two-week period where there was absolutely no interest or pleasure at all,” I don’t know. I don’t feel comfortable saying yes to that. There may have been little moments where I felt something, or where I thought maybe I should care, even if I still didn’t follow through. So… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I feel like I’m falling apart, honestly. Ever since the foreclosure notices started showing up, I just… I haven’t been myself at all. I’ll be sitting in my truck before work crying, trying to pull it together before I go into the shop, and then the phone rings and I jump like something terrible is about to happen.\n\nAt work, I’m snapping at the apprentices over little things, and that’s not how I usually am. I work on heavy equipment, so you can’t be distracted or careless, and I’ve been making dumb mistakes in the repair bay—mistakes I never would’ve made before all this started. It’s like my nerves are shot and I can’t get my footing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried most days, even when there isn’t a specific problem happening at that moment?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it feels like it’s most days now. Ever since those foreclosure notices started coming, I feel like I’m just coming apart at the seams. Even if nothing is happening right that second, I’m still sitting there waiting for the next bad thing. Like I’ll be in my truck before work and I just start crying, trying to get myself together before I walk into the shop.\n\nAnd then the phone rings and I jump like it’s going to be more terrible news. I know that probably sounds ridiculous, but my whole body reacts before I can even think. At work I’m on edge, too. I’ve been snapping at the apprentices over little stuff, and I hate that because that’s not really me. And I’m making stupid mistakes in the repair bay—things I never would’ve messed up before all this. It’s like I can’t shut the worry off, even when there isn’t an actual problem happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or hopeless most days, or have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not just that I feel sad, it’s more like everything that used to keep me steady has just kind of fallen away. I used to be really involved outside of work — church volunteering, my fitness classes, even the truck-club outings. Those were things I showed up for without even thinking about it, like they were part of who I was. Lately I just… don’t go. I’ll tell myself I’m going to, and then I don’t get out the door.\n\nAnd at work it’s gotten bad enough that they’ve taken me out of the high-risk repair bay, which was embarrassing. I’ve always been trusted with that kind of work, and now I’m not. That really hit me hard. So yeah, I guess I’ve lost interest, or maybe I just don’t have whatever it takes right now to keep doing the things I used to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down or hopeless nearly every day for most of the day, and this lasted for at least two weeks straight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I’ve definitely had stretches where I felt really low and just… heavy, like I couldn’t see how things were going to get better. But “nearly every day for most of the day” and “two weeks straight” — I don’t know if I can say that exactly.\n\nSome days it’s there from the minute I wake up, and it follows me around all day. Other days I’ll get distracted for a little while or I’ll be functional enough to get through what I have to do, and then it hits me again later. So I don’t want to overstate it and say yes if you mean literally most of the day every day for two full weeks. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. It’s been bad, but I’m not sure it fits that exact pattern.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, was there ever a time when you felt down or hopeless almost every day, all day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know if I can say that, not the way you’re wording it. I mean, I can look back and say there were times I felt really down, like it was hanging over me most of the time, and I’d wake up already dreading the day. But “almost every day, all day, for two weeks in a row” feels hard for me to pin down.\n\nI wasn’t exactly keeping track, you know? Some days felt completely awful, and then other days maybe I’d have a few hours where I was distracted or just pushing through, and I don’t know whether that counts or not. So I don’t want to say yes and make it sound more exact than I really remember. It’s been bad, but I’m not sure I can honestly say it was all day, nearly every day, for a full two weeks straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer get pleasure or satisfaction out of things you used to enjoy, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings?\n\nPatient: Yes… yeah, I have. Those exact things, actually. I used to get something out of them, not just because they were “activities,” but because they made me feel like myself. Church volunteering gave me a sense that I was useful, like I was showing up for people. Fitness classes made me feel strong and steady. And the truck-club outings were just… fun, you know? Being around people who understood that side of me, talking engines and builds and just having something to look forward to.\n\nNow I mostly don’t go. I keep meaning to. I’ll think, “Okay, this week I’m going to get back to class,” or “I should show up at church, people are probably wondering where I am,” but then when it’s time, it just feels empty or like too much effort for something I’m not even sure I’ll enjoy. Same with the truck-club stuff — I used to be excited about it, and now I’ll see messages about an outing and just let it pass.\n\nAnd it’s not only outside work. At work they’ve taken me out of the high-risk repair bay, and that’s been really hard because that was work I was trusted with. I took pride in that. So losing that, and then not showing up for the things I used to care about after work… it makes me feel like I’ve kind of dropped out of my own life.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, was there ever a period when you felt down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row, even if you can’t say it was absolutely every single day?\n\nPatient: I still don’t know that I can answer that with a clean yes. I understand you’re giving me a little more room there, like not absolutely every single day, but even then… I’m hesitant.\n\nThere have been periods where it felt like I was down most of the time, where the day had this gray cast over it and I was kind of dragging myself through. And if you asked me in the middle of one of those weeks, I probably would’ve said, “Yeah, I’m pretty much miserable.” But looking back and trying to say it was most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks in a row — I’m just not sure.\n\nI can remember bad days grouped together. I can remember waking up already feeling defeated. But I can also remember times where I’d get pulled into something or have a stretch where I was functioning, or not thinking about it as much, and then later I’d crash back into feeling awful. So I don’t know how to count that.\n\nI don’t want to dodge the question. It’s been really low at times. But I don’t feel confident saying, “Yes, definitely, that happened for at least two weeks straight.” I just don’t have that level of certainty about it.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you completely stopped getting any enjoyment or satisfaction from things you used to really like, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings — even for a week or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. Maybe “completely” is a strong word, because there might be little moments where I’m distracted or I can appreciate something in theory, but for a week or more at a time, yes — the things that used to actually give me satisfaction just haven’t felt worth showing up for.\n\nI’ve stopped going to church volunteering, and that bothers me because I used to be pretty consistent with it. It wasn’t just something on the calendar — it made me feel useful, like I was part of something. Same with fitness classes. I used to keep up with them, and now I just don’t go. And the truck-club outings… I used to really look forward to those, and now I’ll see the messages or know there’s something happening, and I just let it pass.\n\nAnd work is part of it too. I’ve been taken out of the high-risk repair bay, and that has hit me hard. That was the kind of work I took pride in, where I felt trusted and capable. So between that and not showing up for the things outside of work that used to matter to me, it really does feel like I’ve lost a lot of what made me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you felt absolutely no interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings, for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “absolutely no” for two weeks or more. That wording is hard for me, because I don’t know that I’ve been completely flat, like there was zero interest or zero pleasure every single day for that long.\n\nWhat I can say is I’ve had stretches where those things felt so distant from me that I just didn’t go, or I didn’t want to go enough to make myself do it. Like I’d see something about the truck-club outing and instead of feeling that pull — the “oh, that’ll be fun” feeling — it was more like, “I can’t deal with that.” Or with fitness classes, I’d think about going and then it would feel pointless before I even got my shoes on. Same with volunteering — I’d know it mattered, but I didn’t feel connected to it the way I used to.\n\nBut if you’re asking specifically, “Was there a solid two-week period where there was absolutely no interest or pleasure at all,” I don’t know. I don’t feel comfortable saying yes to that. There may have been little moments where I felt something, or where I thought maybe I should care, even if I still didn’t follow through. So… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Looking back, was there ever a period when you felt down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row, even if you can’t say it was absolutely every single day?\n\nPatient: I still don’t know that I can answer that with a clean yes. I understand you’re giving me a little more room there, like not absolutely every single day, but even then… I’m hesitant.\n\nThere have been periods where it felt like I was down most of the time, where the day had this gray cast over it and I was kind of dragging myself through. And if you asked me in the middle of one of those weeks, I probably would’ve said, “Yeah, I’m pretty much miserable.” But looking back and trying to say it was most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks in a row — I’m just not sure.\n\nI can remember bad days grouped together. I can remember waking up already feeling defeated. But I can also remember times where I’d get pulled into something or have a stretch where I was functioning, or not thinking about it as much, and then later I’d crash back into feeling awful. So I don’t know how to count that.\n\nI don’t want to dodge the question. It’s been really low at times. But I don’t feel confident saying, “Yes, definitely, that happened for at least two weeks straight.” I just don’t have that level of certainty about it.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you completely stopped getting any enjoyment or satisfaction from things you used to really like, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings — even for a week or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. Maybe “completely” is a strong word, because there might be little moments where I’m distracted or I can appreciate something in theory, but for a week or more at a time, yes — the things that used to actually give me satisfaction just haven’t felt worth showing up for.\n\nI’ve stopped going to church volunteering, and that bothers me because I used to be pretty consistent with it. It wasn’t just something on the calendar — it made me feel useful, like I was part of something. Same with fitness classes. I used to keep up with them, and now I just don’t go. And the truck-club outings… I used to really look forward to those, and now I’ll see the messages or know there’s something happening, and I just let it pass.\n\nAnd work is part of it too. I’ve been taken out of the high-risk repair bay, and that has hit me hard. That was the kind of work I took pride in, where I felt trusted and capable. So between that and not showing up for the things outside of work that used to matter to me, it really does feel like I’ve lost a lot of what made me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you felt absolutely no interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings, for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “absolutely no” for two weeks or more. That wording is hard for me, because I don’t know that I’ve been completely flat, like there was zero interest or zero pleasure every single day for that long.\n\nWhat I can say is I’ve had stretches where those things felt so distant from me that I just didn’t go, or I didn’t want to go enough to make myself do it. Like I’d see something about the truck-club outing and instead of feeling that pull — the “oh, that’ll be fun” feeling — it was more like, “I can’t deal with that.” Or with fitness classes, I’d think about going and then it would feel pointless before I even got my shoes on. Same with volunteering — I’d know it mattered, but I didn’t feel connected to it the way I used to.\n\nBut if you’re asking specifically, “Was there a solid two-week period where there was absolutely no interest or pleasure at all,” I don’t know. I don’t feel comfortable saying yes to that. There may have been little moments where I felt something, or where I thought maybe I should care, even if I still didn’t follow through. So… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you think of any time when you felt no interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings, for a full two weeks without improvement?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / heavy_vehicle_or_mobile_equipment_service_technician_or_mechanic / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’ve been taken out of the high-risk repair bay at work, and I’ve stopped showing up for church volunteering, fitness classes, and truck-club outings I used to keep up with.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess the clearest way I can answer is that I don’t know if I can measure it perfectly as “a full two weeks with no improvement,” but I can tell you the impact has been pretty obvious.

I’ve stopped showing up for things I used to be pretty steady with. Church volunteering, for one — I used to go and feel like I was useful there, like people counted on me, and now I just… don’t go. Fitness classes too. I used to keep those as part of my routine, and I’ve fallen off from them. And the truck-club outings, those were something I actually looked forward to, not just something to fill time, and now I let them pass even when I know they’re happening.

At work, it’s shown up too. I’ve been taken out of the high-risk repair bay, and that’s been really hard to swallow because that was the work where I felt competent and trusted. So whether or not I can say there was absolutely no pleasure for exactly two weeks, I can say I’ve pulled back from a lot of the things that used to matter to me, and it’s affected my work and my life outside of work pretty badly.

I mean, I can look back and say there were times I felt really down, like it was hanging over me most of the time, and I’d wake up already dreading the day. But “almost every day, all day, for two weeks in a row” feels hard for me to pin down.\n\nI wasn’t exactly keeping track, you know? Some days felt completely awful, and then other days maybe I’d have a few hours where I was distracted or just pushing through, and I don’t know whether that counts or not. So I don’t want to say yes and make it sound more exact than I really remember. Being around people who understood that side of me, talking engines and builds and just having something to look forward to.\n\nNow I mostly don’t go. I keep meaning to. I’ll think, “Okay, this week I’m going to get back to class,” or “I should show up at church, people are probably wondering where I am,” but then when it’s time, it just feels empty or like too much effort for something I’m not even sure I’ll enjoy. Same with the truck-club stuff — I used to be excited about it, and now I’ll see messages about an outing and just let it pass.\n\nAnd it’s not only outside work. At work they’ve taken me out of the high-risk repair bay, and that’s been really hard because that was work I was trusted with. I took pride in that. So losing that, and then not showing up for the things I used to care about after work… it makes me feel like I’ve kind of dropped out of my own life.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, was there ever a period when you felt down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row, even if you can’t say it was absolutely every single day?\n\nPatient: I still don’t know that I can answer that with a clean yes. I’m not sure I can say that, no — not the way you’re wording it. “Nearly all activities,” “most of the day,” and “more days than not for at least two weeks” feels very specific, and I don’t know that I can honestly pin it down like that.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, I think I can say that. Maybe not in the perfect way you’re asking with exact days marked off on a calendar, but there have definitely been several days in a row where I just couldn’t get myself to care enough to do the things I normally would’ve cared about.

It’s not just that I missed one fitness class because I was tired, or skipped one truck-club outing because something came up. It’s more like I’d see the reminder or know it was happening, and instead of feeling interested, I’d just think, “What’s the point?” or “I can’t deal with being around people.” And then I wouldn’t go. Same with church volunteering — I used to keep up with that, and it mattered to me, but I’ve stopped showing up there too.

And at work, it’s been hard because I’ve been taken out of the high-risk repair bay. That was a big part of how I saw myself there, like I was trusted to handle the serious stuff, and now not being in that role makes everything feel even more… I don’t know, like I’m not really myself. So yes, there have been stretches of several days where things that used to feel worth doing just didn’t feel worth the effort.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.22, F32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I feel like I’m falling apart, honestly. Ever since the foreclosure notices started showing up, I just… I haven’t been myself at all. I’ll be sitting in my truck before work crying, trying to pull it together before I go into the shop, and then the phone rings and I jump like something terrible is about to happen.\n\nAt work, I’m snapping at the apprentices over little things, and that’s not how I usually am. I work on heavy equipment, so you can’t be distracted or careless, and I’ve been making dumb mistakes in the repair bay—mistakes I never would’ve made before all this started. It’s like my nerves are shot and I can’t get my footing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried most days, even when there isn’t a specific problem happening at that moment?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it feels like it’s most days now. Ever since those foreclosure notices started coming, I feel like I’m just coming apart at the seams. Even if nothing is happening right that second, I’m still sitting there waiting for the next bad thing. Like I’ll be in my truck before work and I just start crying, trying to get myself together before I walk into the shop.\n\nAnd then the phone rings and I jump like it’s going to be more terrible news. I know that probably sounds ridiculous, but my whole body reacts before I can even think. At work I’m on edge, too. I’ve been snapping at the apprentices over little stuff, and I hate that because that’s not really me. And I’m making stupid mistakes in the repair bay—things I never would’ve messed up before all this. It’s like I can’t shut the worry off, even when there isn’t an actual problem happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or hopeless most days, or have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not just that I feel sad, it’s more like everything that used to keep me steady has just kind of fallen away. I used to be really involved outside of work — church volunteering, my fitness classes, even the truck-club outings. Those were things I showed up for without even thinking about it, like they were part of who I was. Lately I just… don’t go. I’ll tell myself I’m going to, and then I don’t get out the door.\n\nAnd at work it’s gotten bad enough that they’ve taken me out of the high-risk repair bay, which was embarrassing. I’ve always been trusted with that kind of work, and now I’m not. That really hit me hard. So yeah, I guess I’ve lost interest, or maybe I just don’t have whatever it takes right now to keep doing the things I used to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down or hopeless nearly every day for most of the day, and this lasted for at least two weeks straight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I’ve definitely had stretches where I felt really low and just… heavy, like I couldn’t see how things were going to get better. But “nearly every day for most of the day” and “two weeks straight” — I don’t know if I can say that exactly.\n\nSome days it’s there from the minute I wake up, and it follows me around all day. Other days I’ll get distracted for a little while or I’ll be functional enough to get through what I have to do, and then it hits me again later. So I don’t want to overstate it and say yes if you mean literally most of the day every day for two full weeks. I haven’t really been tracking it that way. It’s been bad, but I’m not sure it fits that exact pattern.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, was there ever a time when you felt down or hopeless almost every day, all day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know if I can say that, not the way you’re wording it. I mean, I can look back and say there were times I felt really down, like it was hanging over me most of the time, and I’d wake up already dreading the day. But “almost every day, all day, for two weeks in a row” feels hard for me to pin down.\n\nI wasn’t exactly keeping track, you know? Some days felt completely awful, and then other days maybe I’d have a few hours where I was distracted or just pushing through, and I don’t know whether that counts or not. So I don’t want to say yes and make it sound more exact than I really remember. It’s been bad, but I’m not sure I can honestly say it was all day, nearly every day, for a full two weeks straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer get pleasure or satisfaction out of things you used to enjoy, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings?\n\nPatient: Yes… yeah, I have. Those exact things, actually. I used to get something out of them, not just because they were “activities,” but because they made me feel like myself. Church volunteering gave me a sense that I was useful, like I was showing up for people. Fitness classes made me feel strong and steady. And the truck-club outings were just… fun, you know? Being around people who understood that side of me, talking engines and builds and just having something to look forward to.\n\nNow I mostly don’t go. I keep meaning to. I’ll think, “Okay, this week I’m going to get back to class,” or “I should show up at church, people are probably wondering where I am,” but then when it’s time, it just feels empty or like too much effort for something I’m not even sure I’ll enjoy. Same with the truck-club stuff — I used to be excited about it, and now I’ll see messages about an outing and just let it pass.\n\nAnd it’s not only outside work. At work they’ve taken me out of the high-risk repair bay, and that’s been really hard because that was work I was trusted with. I took pride in that. So losing that, and then not showing up for the things I used to care about after work… it makes me feel like I’ve kind of dropped out of my own life.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, was there ever a period when you felt down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row, even if you can’t say it was absolutely every single day?\n\nPatient: I still don’t know that I can answer that with a clean yes. I understand you’re giving me a little more room there, like not absolutely every single day, but even then… I’m hesitant.\n\nThere have been periods where it felt like I was down most of the time, where the day had this gray cast over it and I was kind of dragging myself through. And if you asked me in the middle of one of those weeks, I probably would’ve said, “Yeah, I’m pretty much miserable.” But looking back and trying to say it was most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks in a row — I’m just not sure.\n\nI can remember bad days grouped together. I can remember waking up already feeling defeated. But I can also remember times where I’d get pulled into something or have a stretch where I was functioning, or not thinking about it as much, and then later I’d crash back into feeling awful. So I don’t know how to count that.\n\nI don’t want to dodge the question. It’s been really low at times. But I don’t feel confident saying, “Yes, definitely, that happened for at least two weeks straight.” I just don’t have that level of certainty about it.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you completely stopped getting any enjoyment or satisfaction from things you used to really like, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings — even for a week or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. Maybe “completely” is a strong word, because there might be little moments where I’m distracted or I can appreciate something in theory, but for a week or more at a time, yes — the things that used to actually give me satisfaction just haven’t felt worth showing up for.\n\nI’ve stopped going to church volunteering, and that bothers me because I used to be pretty consistent with it. It wasn’t just something on the calendar — it made me feel useful, like I was part of something. Same with fitness classes. I used to keep up with them, and now I just don’t go. And the truck-club outings… I used to really look forward to those, and now I’ll see the messages or know there’s something happening, and I just let it pass.\n\nAnd work is part of it too. I’ve been taken out of the high-risk repair bay, and that has hit me hard. That was the kind of work I took pride in, where I felt trusted and capable. So between that and not showing up for the things outside of work that used to matter to me, it really does feel like I’ve lost a lot of what made me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you felt absolutely no interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings, for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “absolutely no” for two weeks or more. That wording is hard for me, because I don’t know that I’ve been completely flat, like there was zero interest or zero pleasure every single day for that long.\n\nWhat I can say is I’ve had stretches where those things felt so distant from me that I just didn’t go, or I didn’t want to go enough to make myself do it. Like I’d see something about the truck-club outing and instead of feeling that pull — the “oh, that’ll be fun” feeling — it was more like, “I can’t deal with that.” Or with fitness classes, I’d think about going and then it would feel pointless before I even got my shoes on. Same with volunteering — I’d know it mattered, but I didn’t feel connected to it the way I used to.\n\nBut if you’re asking specifically, “Was there a solid two-week period where there was absolutely no interest or pleasure at all,” I don’t know. I don’t feel comfortable saying yes to that. There may have been little moments where I felt something, or where I thought maybe I should care, even if I still didn’t follow through. So… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when you felt no interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings, for a full two weeks without improvement?\n\nPatient: I guess the clearest way I can answer is that I don’t know if I can measure it perfectly as “a full two weeks with no improvement,” but I can tell you the impact has been pretty obvious.\n\nI’ve stopped showing up for things I used to be pretty steady with. Church volunteering, for one — I used to go and feel like I was useful there, like people counted on me, and now I just… don’t go. Fitness classes too. I used to keep those as part of my routine, and I’ve fallen off from them. And the truck-club outings, those were something I actually looked forward to, not just something to fill time, and now I let them pass even when I know they’re happening.\n\nAt work, it’s shown up too. I’ve been taken out of the high-risk repair bay, and that’s been really hard to swallow because that was the work where I felt competent and trusted. So whether or not I can say there was absolutely no pleasure for exactly two weeks, I can say I’ve pulled back from a lot of the things that used to matter to me, and it’s affected my work and my life outside of work pretty badly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period where you lost interest or pleasure in nearly all activities, not just the ones you mentioned, for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no — not the way you’re wording it. “Nearly all activities,” “most of the day,” and “more days than not for at least two weeks” feels very specific, and I don’t know that I can honestly pin it down like that.\n\nI know I’ve pulled away from the big things that used to matter to me, like the ones we talked about. And I know there have been days where I just don’t feel much pull toward anything, where even normal stuff feels kind of… flat or like too much effort. But I don’t know if that was nearly everything, most of the day, for a solid two weeks.\n\nIt’s more that my interest has been really inconsistent and a lot lower than it used to be. Some things I avoid, some things I think about doing but don’t follow through with, and maybe here and there I still get a small bit of interest or comfort from something. So I wouldn’t want to say yes if you mean a clear two-week stretch where almost nothing felt enjoyable most of the day. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you felt little or no interest in things you used to care about, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings, and this lasted for several days in a row — not just one day or a few hours, but several days where nothing really felt worth doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think I can say that. Maybe not in the perfect way you’re asking with exact days marked off on a calendar, but there have definitely been several days in a row where I just couldn’t get myself to care enough to do the things I normally would’ve cared about.\n\nIt’s not just that I missed one fitness class because I was tired, or skipped one truck-club outing because something came up. It’s more like I’d see the reminder or know it was happening, and instead of feeling interested, I’d just think, “What’s the point?” or “I can’t deal with being around people.” And then I wouldn’t go. Same with church volunteering — I used to keep up with that, and it mattered to me, but I’ve stopped showing up there too.\n\nAnd at work, it’s been hard because I’ve been taken out of the high-risk repair bay. That was a big part of how I saw myself there, like I was trusted to handle the serious stuff, and now not being in that role makes everything feel even more… I don’t know, like I’m not really myself. So yes, there have been stretches of several days where things that used to feel worth doing just didn’t feel worth the effort.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I still don’t know that I can say that clearly, even looking back. I mean, I can tell you there were stretches where I was really withdrawn and things I normally cared about didn’t seem worth the effort, but when you say “a continuous period of at least two weeks” and “most things,” I get stuck.

So yes, there have been stretches of several days where things that used to feel worth doing just didn’t feel worth the effort.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, was there ever a continuous period of at least two weeks where you felt a clear loss of interest or pleasure in most things you used to enjoy, even if it wasn’t every single day of that time?\n\nPatient: I still don’t know that I can say that clearly, even looking back. I mean, I can tell you there were stretches where I was really withdrawn and things I normally cared about didn’t seem worth the effort, but when you say “a continuous period of at least two weeks” and “most things,” I get stuck.\n\nPart of me wants to say maybe, because it has felt like this has gone on and on, and I’ve definitely pulled away from a lot. But if I’m being accurate, I don’t have a neat two-week block in my head where I can say, yes, during that whole time I had a clear loss of interest or pleasure in most things. It’s more uneven than that. Some days are really flat, some days I can still get a little interested in something or feel okay for a bit, and then it drops off again.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’m not sure. I’ve had several-day stretches where things didn’t feel worth doing, but I can’t honestly say I remember a continuous two weeks like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nLooking back, has there been a period of at least two weeks where you generally lost interest or pleasure in most of the things you used to enjoy, even if there were some small moments of interest or pleasure during that time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / heavy_vehicle_or_mobile_equipment_service_technician_or_mechanic / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m still hesitant to say yes to that in a clean way. I know you’re making room for “small moments,” which helps, but even then… I don’t have a clear two-week stretch in my mind where I can confidently say I generally lost interest or pleasure in most things.

It’s more like I’ve had runs of days where I’m really checked out, where the things I normally care about just don’t pull me at all. And then there might be a little window where something feels okay, or I think maybe I’ll do something, and then I don’t follow through, or the feeling fades. So when I look back, it feels messy and uneven, not like a solid block of time I can point to.

I don’t want to overstate it just because things have been bad. I can say my interest has dropped a lot, and I’ve been avoiding a lot of things I used to enjoy. But a definite “at least two weeks, most things, generally lost interest or pleasure” — I’m not sure I can honestly say that.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.22, F32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I feel like I’m falling apart, honestly. Ever since the foreclosure notices started showing up, I just… I haven’t been myself at all. I’ll be sitting in my truck before work crying, trying to pull it together before I go into the shop, and then the phone rings and I jump like something terrible is about to happen.\n\nAt work, I’m snapping at the apprentices over little things, and that’s not how I usually am. I work on heavy equipment, so you can’t be distracted or careless, and I’ve been making dumb mistakes in the repair bay—mistakes I never would’ve made before all this started. It’s like my nerves are shot and I can’t get my footing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried most days, even when there isn’t a specific problem happening at that moment?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it feels like it’s most days now. Ever since those foreclosure notices started coming, I feel like I’m just coming apart at the seams. Even if nothing is happening right that second, I’m still sitting there waiting for the next bad thing. Like I’ll be in my truck before work and I just start crying, trying to get myself together before I walk into the shop.\n\nAnd then the phone rings and I jump like it’s going to be more terrible news. And I’m making stupid mistakes in the repair bay—things I never would’ve messed up before all this. It’s like I can’t shut the worry off, even when there isn’t an actual problem happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or hopeless most days, or have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I used to be really involved outside of work — church volunteering, my fitness classes, even the truck-club outings. I’ve always been trusted with that kind of work, and now I’m not. That really hit me hard. So yeah, I guess I’ve lost interest, or maybe I just don’t have whatever it takes right now to keep doing the things I used to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down or hopeless nearly every day for most of the day, and this lasted for at least two weeks straight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I’ve definitely had stretches where I felt really low and just… heavy, like I couldn’t see how things were going to get better. But “nearly every day for most of the day” and “two weeks straight” — I don’t know if I can say that exactly.\n\nSome days it’s there from the minute I wake up, and it follows me around all day. Other days I’ll get distracted for a little while or I’ll be functional enough to get through what I have to do, and then it hits me again later. It’s been bad, but I’m not sure it fits that exact pattern.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, was there ever a time when you felt down or hopeless almost every day, all day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know if I can say that, not the way you’re wording it. I mean, I can look back and say there were times I felt really down, like it was hanging over me most of the time, and I’d wake up already dreading the day. But “almost every day, all day, for two weeks in a row” feels hard for me to pin down.\n\nI wasn’t exactly keeping track, you know? Some days felt completely awful, and then other days maybe I’d have a few hours where I was distracted or just pushing through, and I don’t know whether that counts or not. So I don’t want to say yes and make it sound more exact than I really remember. It’s been bad, but I’m not sure I can honestly say it was all day, nearly every day, for a full two weeks straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer get pleasure or satisfaction out of things you used to enjoy, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings?\n\nPatient: Yes… yeah, I have. Those exact things, actually. I used to get something out of them, not just because they were “activities,” but because they made me feel like myself. Church volunteering gave me a sense that I was useful, like I was showing up for people. Fitness classes made me feel strong and steady. And the truck-club outings were just… fun, you know? Being around people who understood that side of me, talking engines and builds and just having something to look forward to.\n\nNow I mostly don’t go. I keep meaning to. I’ll think, “Okay, this week I’m going to get back to class,” or “I should show up at church, people are probably wondering where I am,” but then when it’s time, it just feels empty or like too much effort for something I’m not even sure I’ll enjoy. Same with the truck-club stuff — I used to be excited about it, and now I’ll see messages about an outing and just let it pass.\n\nAnd it’s not only outside work. At work they’ve taken me out of the high-risk repair bay, and that’s been really hard because that was work I was trusted with. I took pride in that. So losing that, and then not showing up for the things I used to care about after work… it makes me feel like I’ve kind of dropped out of my own life.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, was there ever a period when you felt down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row, even if you can’t say it was absolutely every single day?\n\nPatient: I still don’t know that I can answer that with a clean yes. I understand you’re giving me a little more room there, like not absolutely every single day, but even then… I’m hesitant.\n\nThere have been periods where it felt like I was down most of the time, where the day had this gray cast over it and I was kind of dragging myself through. And if you asked me in the middle of one of those weeks, I probably would’ve said, “Yeah, I’m pretty much miserable.” But looking back and trying to say it was most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks in a row — I’m just not sure.\n\nI can remember bad days grouped together. I can remember waking up already feeling defeated. But I can also remember times where I’d get pulled into something or have a stretch where I was functioning, or not thinking about it as much, and then later I’d crash back into feeling awful. So I don’t know how to count that.\n\nI don’t want to dodge the question. It’s been really low at times. But I don’t feel confident saying, “Yes, definitely, that happened for at least two weeks straight.” I just don’t have that level of certainty about it.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you completely stopped getting any enjoyment or satisfaction from things you used to really like, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings — even for a week or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. Maybe “completely” is a strong word, because there might be little moments where I’m distracted or I can appreciate something in theory, but for a week or more at a time, yes — the things that used to actually give me satisfaction just haven’t felt worth showing up for.\n\nI’ve stopped going to church volunteering, and that bothers me because I used to be pretty consistent with it. It wasn’t just something on the calendar — it made me feel useful, like I was part of something. Same with fitness classes. I used to keep up with them, and now I just don’t go. And the truck-club outings… I used to really look forward to those, and now I’ll see the messages or know there’s something happening, and I just let it pass.\n\nAnd work is part of it too. I’ve been taken out of the high-risk repair bay, and that has hit me hard. That was the kind of work I took pride in, where I felt trusted and capable. So between that and not showing up for the things outside of work that used to matter to me, it really does feel like I’ve lost a lot of what made me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you felt absolutely no interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings, for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “absolutely no” for two weeks or more. That wording is hard for me, because I don’t know that I’ve been completely flat, like there was zero interest or zero pleasure every single day for that long.\n\nWhat I can say is I’ve had stretches where those things felt so distant from me that I just didn’t go, or I didn’t want to go enough to make myself do it. Like I’d see something about the truck-club outing and instead of feeling that pull — the “oh, that’ll be fun” feeling — it was more like, “I can’t deal with that.” Or with fitness classes, I’d think about going and then it would feel pointless before I even got my shoes on. Same with volunteering — I’d know it mattered, but I didn’t feel connected to it the way I used to.\n\nBut if you’re asking specifically, “Was there a solid two-week period where there was absolutely no interest or pleasure at all,” I don’t know. I don’t feel comfortable saying yes to that. There may have been little moments where I felt something, or where I thought maybe I should care, even if I still didn’t follow through. So… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when you felt no interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings, for a full two weeks without improvement?\n\nPatient: I guess the clearest way I can answer is that I don’t know if I can measure it perfectly as “a full two weeks with no improvement,” but I can tell you the impact has been pretty obvious.\n\nI’ve stopped showing up for things I used to be pretty steady with. Church volunteering, for one — I used to go and feel like I was useful there, like people counted on me, and now I just… don’t go. Fitness classes too. I used to keep those as part of my routine, and I’ve fallen off from them. And the truck-club outings, those were something I actually looked forward to, not just something to fill time, and now I let them pass even when I know they’re happening.\n\nAt work, it’s shown up too. I’ve been taken out of the high-risk repair bay, and that’s been really hard to swallow because that was the work where I felt competent and trusted. So whether or not I can say there was absolutely no pleasure for exactly two weeks, I can say I’ve pulled back from a lot of the things that used to matter to me, and it’s affected my work and my life outside of work pretty badly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period where you lost interest or pleasure in nearly all activities, not just the ones you mentioned, for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no — not the way you’re wording it. “Nearly all activities,” “most of the day,” and “more days than not for at least two weeks” feels very specific, and I don’t know that I can honestly pin it down like that.\n\nI know I’ve pulled away from the big things that used to matter to me, like the ones we talked about. And I know there have been days where I just don’t feel much pull toward anything, where even normal stuff feels kind of… flat or like too much effort. But I don’t know if that was nearly everything, most of the day, for a solid two weeks.\n\nIt’s more that my interest has been really inconsistent and a lot lower than it used to be. Some things I avoid, some things I think about doing but don’t follow through with, and maybe here and there I still get a small bit of interest or comfort from something. So I wouldn’t want to say yes if you mean a clear two-week stretch where almost nothing felt enjoyable most of the day. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you felt little or no interest in things you used to care about, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings, and this lasted for several days in a row — not just one day or a few hours, but several days where nothing really felt worth doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think I can say that. Maybe not in the perfect way you’re asking with exact days marked off on a calendar, but there have definitely been several days in a row where I just couldn’t get myself to care enough to do the things I normally would’ve cared about.\n\nIt’s not just that I missed one fitness class because I was tired, or skipped one truck-club outing because something came up. It’s more like I’d see the reminder or know it was happening, and instead of feeling interested, I’d just think, “What’s the point?” or “I can’t deal with being around people.” And then I wouldn’t go. Same with church volunteering — I used to keep up with that, and it mattered to me, but I’ve stopped showing up there too.\n\nAnd at work, it’s been hard because I’ve been taken out of the high-risk repair bay. That was a big part of how I saw myself there, like I was trusted to handle the serious stuff, and now not being in that role makes everything feel even more… I don’t know, like I’m not really myself. So yes, there have been stretches of several days where things that used to feel worth doing just didn’t feel worth the effort.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, was there ever a continuous period of at least two weeks where you felt a clear loss of interest or pleasure in most things you used to enjoy, even if it wasn’t every single day of that time?\n\nPatient: I still don’t know that I can say that clearly, even looking back. I mean, I can tell you there were stretches where I was really withdrawn and things I normally cared about didn’t seem worth the effort, but when you say “a continuous period of at least two weeks” and “most things,” I get stuck.\n\nPart of me wants to say maybe, because it has felt like this has gone on and on, and I’ve definitely pulled away from a lot. Maybe “completely” is a strong word, because there might be little moments where I’m distracted or I can appreciate something in theory, but for a week or more at a time, yes — the things that used to actually give me satisfaction just haven’t felt worth showing up for.\n\nI’ve stopped going to church volunteering, and that bothers me because I used to be pretty consistent with it. It wasn’t just something on the calendar — it made me feel useful, like I was part of something. Same with fitness classes. I used to keep up with them, and now I just don’t go. And the truck-club outings… I used to really look forward to those, and now I’ll see the messages or know there’s something happening, and I just let it pass.\n\nAnd work is part of it too. I’ve been taken out of the high-risk repair bay, and that has hit me hard. That was the kind of work I took pride in, where I felt trusted and capable. So between that and not showing up for the things outside of work that used to matter to me, it really does feel like I’ve lost a lot of what made me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you felt absolutely no interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings, for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “absolutely no” for two weeks or more. That wording is hard for me, because I don’t know that I’ve been completely flat, like there was zero interest or zero pleasure every single day for that long.\n\nWhat I can say is I’ve had stretches where those things felt so distant from me that I just didn’t go, or I didn’t want to go enough to make myself do it. Like I’d see something about the truck-club outing and instead of feeling that pull — the “oh, that’ll be fun” feeling — it was more like, “I can’t deal with that.” Or with fitness classes, I’d think about going and then it would feel pointless before I even got my shoes on. Same with volunteering — I’d know it mattered, but I didn’t feel connected to it the way I used to.\n\nBut if you’re asking specifically, “Was there a solid two-week period where there was absolutely no interest or pleasure at all,” I don’t know. I don’t feel comfortable saying yes to that. There may have been little moments where I felt something, or where I thought maybe I should care, even if I still didn’t follow through. So… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when you felt no interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings, for a full two weeks without improvement?\n\nPatient: I guess the clearest way I can answer is that I don’t know if I can measure it perfectly as “a full two weeks with no improvement,” but I can tell you the impact has been pretty obvious.\n\nI’ve stopped showing up for things I used to be pretty steady with. Church volunteering, for one — I used to go and feel like I was useful there, like people counted on me, and now I just… don’t go. Fitness classes too. I used to keep those as part of my routine, and I’ve fallen off from them. And the truck-club outings, those were something I actually looked forward to, not just something to fill time, and now I let them pass even when I know they’re happening.\n\nAt work, it’s shown up too. I’ve been taken out of the high-risk repair bay, and that’s been really hard to swallow because that was the work where I felt competent and trusted. So whether or not I can say there was absolutely no pleasure for exactly two weeks, I can say I’ve pulled back from a lot of the things that used to matter to me, and it’s affected my work and my life outside of work pretty badly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period where you lost interest or pleasure in nearly all activities, not just the ones you mentioned, for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no — not the way you’re wording it. “Nearly all activities,” “most of the day,” and “more days than not for at least two weeks” feels very specific, and I don’t know that I can honestly pin it down like that.\n\nI know I’ve pulled away from the big things that used to matter to me, like the ones we talked about. And I know there have been days where I just don’t feel much pull toward anything, where even normal stuff feels kind of… flat or like too much effort. But I don’t know if that was nearly everything, most of the day, for a solid two weeks.\n\nIt’s more that my interest has been really inconsistent and a lot lower than it used to be. Some things I avoid, some things I think about doing but don’t follow through with, and maybe here and there I still get a small bit of interest or comfort from something. So I wouldn’t want to say yes if you mean a clear two-week stretch where almost nothing felt enjoyable most of the day. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you felt little or no interest in things you used to care about, like your volunteer work, fitness classes, or truck-club outings, and this lasted for several days in a row — not just one day or a few hours, but several days where nothing really felt worth doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think I can say that. Maybe not in the perfect way you’re asking with exact days marked off on a calendar, but there have definitely been several days in a row where I just couldn’t get myself to care enough to do the things I normally would’ve cared about.\n\nIt’s not just that I missed one fitness class because I was tired, or skipped one truck-club outing because something came up. I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve definitely felt low, and there have been days where it feels like it sits on me for a lot of the day, not just a little passing mood. But when you say “most of the day, more often than not, for at least two weeks,” I start trying to line it up in my head and I don’t know that I can honestly pin it down like that.

It hasn’t felt simple or steady, if that makes sense. There are days where I wake up already feeling kind of sunk, and it follows me through work or through the evening, and then there might be a better patch where I’m distracted or I feel more like myself for a bit. Then later it can come back. So looking back, it all blends together, but I can’t confidently say, yes, there was a definite two-week period where I was down most of the day on most days.

I don’t want to make it sound lighter than it is, because it has been rough. But I also don’t want to say something too definite just because I’m upset sitting here talking about it. So I guess my honest answer is: maybe, but I’m not sure. I know I’ve had a lot of low days. I just can’t clearly say it met that exact two-week pattern.
